I’ll end up turning a little cable tie or some random bit of plastic in my fingers over and over until someone gives me that look, like, “Can you stop?” Then I’ll realize I’ve been doing it nonstop. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive; I just feel kind of wound up if I’m sitting there with nothing moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. I catch myself cutting people off, especially when it’s anything technical. Someone will be describing a computer or network problem, and before they’ve even finished the second half of the sentence, my brain has already jumped to, “Oh, I know what that is,” and then I’m talking over them.\n\nIt’s not usually meant to be rude—I’m not trying to dominate the conversation—but the answer feels like it’s right there and I feel this pressure to say it before it disappears or before the conversation moves on. Sometimes I’ll even reach for the keyboard or mouse while they’re still explaining what happened, like I’m already trying to fix it before I’ve actually heard the full story.\n\nAnd then, of course, sometimes I’m wrong because I didn’t let them finish. That’s the part that makes me feel pretty bad. I know better. I work in support, so listening carefully is supposed to be part of the job. But in the moment I just jump in.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Honestly, that’s become one of the hardest parts of all of this, because it’s not just “I’m distracted at work” anymore. It’s affected how people see me.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I could kind of lean on the routines and systems around me. Since losing that structure, things have gotten a lot more obvious. I’ve been doing freelance work, and I’ve lost clients because I didn’t follow through the way I should have. Not because I didn’t want to, but because I’d miss something, or delay responding, or let a task sit too long, and then by the time I tried to recover it, the trust was already damaged.\n\nMy reputation has really taken a hit. People who used to think of me as reliable don’t really see me that way now, and that hurts. I also missed out on a senior service-manager role, and I can’t help feeling like this played a big part in it—people just don’t trust me to stay on top of things consistently.\n\nAnd at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because of it. That’s been… pretty upsetting. It’s one thing for me to feel frustrated with myself, but when it affects her too, and our stability, that makes me feel awful. So yes, outside of the actual job tasks, it’s caused real problems in daily life and in how people relate to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and impulsivity, or is this something that has developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not new. I mean, it’s definitely gotten more obvious as an adult, especially when I don’t have structure around me, but looking back, this has been there since I was a kid.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” — bright but doesn’t stay in his seat, bright but blurts out answers, bright but loses his homework. And the maddening part is, sometimes I had actually done the homework. I just wouldn’t turn it in, or I’d lose it somewhere between my desk, my backpack, and school. So it wasn’t like I suddenly became this way in midlife.\n\nAt the time, I think people just treated it like I was energetic or careless or not applying myself, because I could understand the material. But the focus and the impulse to speak before thinking — that was already there. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not just annoying or inconvenient — it’s been really distressing because it’s changed how people see me and what they’re willing to trust me with.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it feels like everything has gotten exposed. With freelance work, if I don’t follow through exactly when I should, or I let something slip, there isn’t really anyone else catching it. And I’ve lost clients because of that. People stop believing I’ll do what I say I’m going to do, even when I genuinely mean to.\n\nMy reputation has taken a real hit, and that bothers me a lot. I missed out on a senior service-manager role, too, and I keep thinking, “Would that have gone differently if people trusted me to stay on top of things?” That’s hard to sit with.\n\nAnd then at home, it’s not just my pride. My wife and I have had to worry about money because of it. That part really gets to me. It’s one thing for me to disappoint myself, but when it affects our stability and she has to live with the consequences too… yes, that causes me significant distress.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these issues when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I don’t remember ever being formally diagnosed with anything as a child, and I don’t think I received any specific treatment for it.\n\nBack then it was more handled as… I don’t know, teachers commenting on it, my parents being frustrated, people saying I needed to try harder or settle down. But I don’t recall anyone sitting down and saying, “This is a condition,” or sending me for therapy or medication or anything like that.\n\nIt was just sort of treated as part of my personality, I guess — like I was bright but careless, or restless, or not living up to my potential. So no, as far as I know, there wasn’t a diagnosis or treatment when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity affected your relationships with friends or family members in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, the biggest impact has been at home, with my wife, because the consequences don’t stay contained to me. Since I lost the structure of my old job and started doing more freelance work, people have stopped trusting me to follow through the way I say I will. And when that happens, it doesn’t just hurt my pride — it affects our money, our stability, and the way we talk to each other.\n\nI’ve lost freelance clients over this, and that was really hard. Not just financially, but because it feels like my reputation has taken a real hit. People start seeing me as unreliable, even when I’m not trying to be. I’ll genuinely mean to do something, and then if I don’t stay on top of it exactly right, the opportunity is gone or the person doesn’t come back.\n\nI also missed out on a senior service-manager role, and that one still bothers me. I keep thinking maybe if people had more confidence in my follow-through, it might have gone differently. And my wife has had to live with the fallout of that too — the stress, the uncertainty, worrying about money. So yes, it’s affected my family in a significant way. It’s embarrassing to say, but I think people close to me have had to carry some of the consequences of me not being seen as dependable.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant periods in your life where these focus and impulsivity issues were not a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Not really — at least not in the way it’s been a problem recently. I mean, looking back, I can’t say I was ever naturally great at keeping myself on track, but the serious trouble part has really been since I was fired and lost the structure that used to hold things together for me.\n\nThat was about… a year and ten months ago now. Almost two years. Before that, there were systems around me — schedules, expectations, other people checking in, routines that kind of forced me to stay aligned. I don’t think I realized how much I depended on that until it was gone.\n\nSince then, it’s been much more obvious and much more damaging. Without those external supports, things started slipping in a way I couldn’t just explain away. So no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant period recently where it hasn’t been a problem. It’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months, ever since that firing and losing the systems that kept me on track.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties with focus and impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. My mother still brings it up, actually — kind of jokingly, but also in that way where you can tell it was a real thing at the time. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers before I was supposed to, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it.\n\nThat last part is the one that still makes me cringe a little, because it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done the work. Sometimes I had, but it would disappear somewhere between my desk, my backpack, home, and school. And I remember the adults around me acting like it was carelessness or laziness, like, “How can you do the assignment and then not turn it in?” Which is fair, I guess, but as a kid I didn’t really know how to explain it.\n\nSo yes, there were definitely comments from teachers, and my mother remembers them pretty clearly. At the time it just got framed as me being smart but not applying myself, or being disruptive, or needing to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently affect you throughout your childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. I mean, I can’t remember every year perfectly, obviously, but it wasn’t like it suddenly appeared as an adult. My mother still jokes about it because it showed up so much in grade school. She’ll say my report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the rest would be about me not staying in my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, or somehow losing homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that makes me feel like it was pretty consistent — it wasn’t just one bad teacher or one bad semester. It was kind of a theme. I was not a kid who just smoothly followed the classroom routine. Adults noticed it enough that it became part of how they described me. Even now, my mother can still repeat those comments back like they were a running joke, but honestly, it sounds like it was frustrating for everyone at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods in your childhood where you felt like you could focus and sit still without any issues?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really remember, no. I mean, I’m sure there were individual moments where I was absorbed in something or behaved fine for a while, but as a general thing in school, it doesn’t sound like I had long stretches where sitting still and focusing were just easy.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually — that my grade school report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it. So it wasn’t like there was this clear period where everyone thought, “Oh, he’s totally fine now.” It seems like it was a pretty steady theme.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months where you did not have any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in any way that feels like a clean break from it. I think the more honest answer is that it’s always been there to some degree, but the part where it became seriously disruptive has been more recent.\n\nIt’s been causing me real trouble for about… a year and ten months now, I’d say. That lines up with when I was fired and lost the structure that had been keeping me on track. Before that, I had systems around me that sort of compensated for it, even if I didn’t think of it that way at the time. Once those were gone, it became much harder to manage, and it started affecting me in a much bigger way. So I can’t point to a six-month period where I had no issues at all — just periods where the problems were more contained because I had enough external structure holding things together.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely not just one place. It’s probably most obvious with work, because that’s where the consequences have been the clearest, but it spills over at home too.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, things have kind of unraveled in a way that’s embarrassing to talk about. My reputation has taken a real hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because I didn’t follow through reliably enough, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I really thought I should have been in the running for. And at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because people just don’t trust me the way they used to to get things done.\n\nSo, yes — work, home, and honestly the social/professional side too, because once people start seeing you as unreliable, it follows you around.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes, from what I understand, it was there pretty early — definitely in grade school. I don’t remember every detail myself, obviously, but my mother still brings it up and kind of jokes about it, like it was this recurring theme on my report cards.\n\nThey’d say I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers instead of waiting my turn. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which is still frustrating to hear because it sounds so familiar. And at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because people just don’t trust me the way they used to to get things done.\n\nSo, yes — work, home, and honestly the social/professional side too, because once people start seeing you as unreliable, it follows you around.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes, from what I understand, it was there pretty early — definitely in grade school. I don’t remember every detail myself, obviously, but my mother still brings it up and kind of jokes about it, like it was this recurring theme on my report cards.\n\nThey’d say I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers instead of waiting my turn. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which is still frustrating to hear because it sounds so familiar.   },

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not disappear, no. I mean… there were definitely times as an adult when it was more manageable, but looking back, I think that had more to do with having systems around me than the symptoms actually going away.

The big change was after I was fired. That was about a year and ten months ago, and since then it’s been causing serious trouble. Before that, the structure of work, routines, other people expecting things from me in a predictable way — all of that kind of held me in place. Once that was gone, it became a lot more obvious how much I was depending on those supports.

So I wouldn’t say there was a period where it really disappeared. It was more like it was contained for a while, and then when those systems were gone, it got much harder to keep things together.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.8, F90.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I’m having a really hard time keeping my mind on things long enough to finish them. It’s gotten to where even important tasks at work are slipping away from me while I’m doing them.\n\nI do computer support, so a lot of my job is tracing problems carefully—looking through logs, following a network issue step by step, keeping track of what I’ve ruled out. And I’ll sit down to work on a client’s network problem, fully intending to focus, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries over and over. Like, I’ve reread the same lines three times, but I still can’t tell you what they actually say or what the next step is supposed to be.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the frustrating part. These are things that matter, and I know they matter while I’m doing them, but my attention just won’t stay put long enough for me to get through the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. It’s almost embarrassing how automatic it is. If I’m sitting with a client and I’m supposed to be listening or explaining something, I’ll suddenly realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time under the desk. Or I’m clicking a pen without even noticing I picked it up.\n\nAnd it happens in meetings too, like at chess club meetings—I’ll be trying to sit still and pay attention, but my hands need something to do. I’ll end up turning a little cable tie or some random bit of plastic in my fingers over and over until someone gives me that look, like, “Can you stop?” Then I’ll realize I’ve been doing it nonstop. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive; I just feel kind of wound up if I’m sitting there with nothing moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. I catch myself cutting people off, especially when it’s anything technical. Someone will be describing a computer or network problem, and before they’ve even finished the second half of the sentence, my brain has already jumped to, “Oh, I know what that is,” and then I’m talking over them.\n\nIt’s not usually meant to be rude—I’m not trying to dominate the conversation—but the answer feels like it’s right there and I feel this pressure to say it before it disappears or before the conversation moves on. Sometimes I’ll even reach for the keyboard or mouse while they’re still explaining what happened, like I’m already trying to fix it before I’ve actually heard the full story.\n\nAnd then, of course, sometimes I’m wrong because I didn’t let them finish. That’s the part that makes me feel pretty bad. I know better. I work in support, so listening carefully is supposed to be part of the job. But in the moment I just jump in.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Honestly, that’s become one of the hardest parts of all of this, because it’s not just “I’m distracted at work” anymore. It’s affected how people see me.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I could kind of lean on the routines and systems around me. Since losing that structure, things have gotten a lot more obvious. I’ve been doing freelance work, and I’ve lost clients because I didn’t follow through the way I should have. Not because I didn’t want to, but because I’d miss something, or delay responding, or let a task sit too long, and then by the time I tried to recover it, the trust was already damaged.\n\nMy reputation has really taken a hit. People who used to think of me as reliable don’t really see me that way now, and that hurts. I also missed out on a senior service-manager role, and I can’t help feeling like this played a big part in it—people just don’t trust me to stay on top of things consistently.\n\nAnd at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because of it. That’s been… pretty upsetting. It’s one thing for me to feel frustrated with myself, but when it affects her too, and our stability, that makes me feel awful. So yes, outside of the actual job tasks, it’s caused real problems in daily life and in how people relate to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and impulsivity, or is this something that has developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not new. I mean, it’s definitely gotten more obvious as an adult, especially when I don’t have structure around me, but looking back, this has been there since I was a kid.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” — bright but doesn’t stay in his seat, bright but blurts out answers, bright but loses his homework. And the maddening part is, sometimes I had actually done the homework. I just wouldn’t turn it in, or I’d lose it somewhere between my desk, my backpack, and school. So it wasn’t like I suddenly became this way in midlife.\n\nAt the time, I think people just treated it like I was energetic or careless or not applying myself, because I could understand the material. But the focus and the impulse to speak before thinking — that was already there. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not just annoying or inconvenient — it’s been really distressing because it’s changed how people see me and what they’re willing to trust me with.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it feels like everything has gotten exposed. With freelance work, if I don’t follow through exactly when I should, or I let something slip, there isn’t really anyone else catching it. And I’ve lost clients because of that. People stop believing I’ll do what I say I’m going to do, even when I genuinely mean to.\n\nMy reputation has taken a real hit, and that bothers me a lot. I missed out on a senior service-manager role, too, and I keep thinking, “Would that have gone differently if people trusted me to stay on top of things?” That’s hard to sit with.\n\nAnd then at home, it’s not just my pride. My wife and I have had to worry about money because of it. That part really gets to me. It’s one thing for me to disappoint myself, but when it affects our stability and she has to live with the consequences too… yes, that causes me significant distress.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these issues when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I don’t remember ever being formally diagnosed with anything as a child, and I don’t think I received any specific treatment for it.\n\nBack then it was more handled as… I don’t know, teachers commenting on it, my parents being frustrated, people saying I needed to try harder or settle down. But I don’t recall anyone sitting down and saying, “This is a condition,” or sending me for therapy or medication or anything like that.\n\nIt was just sort of treated as part of my personality, I guess — like I was bright but careless, or restless, or not living up to my potential. So no, as far as I know, there wasn’t a diagnosis or treatment when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity affected your relationships with friends or family members in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, the biggest impact has been at home, with my wife, because the consequences don’t stay contained to me. Since I lost the structure of my old job and started doing more freelance work, people have stopped trusting me to follow through the way I say I will. And when that happens, it doesn’t just hurt my pride — it affects our money, our stability, and the way we talk to each other.\n\nI’ve lost freelance clients over this, and that was really hard. Not just financially, but because it feels like my reputation has taken a real hit. People start seeing me as unreliable, even when I’m not trying to be. I’ll genuinely mean to do something, and then if I don’t stay on top of it exactly right, the opportunity is gone or the person doesn’t come back.\n\nI also missed out on a senior service-manager role, and that one still bothers me. I keep thinking maybe if people had more confidence in my follow-through, it might have gone differently. And my wife has had to live with the fallout of that too — the stress, the uncertainty, worrying about money. So yes, it’s affected my family in a significant way. It’s embarrassing to say, but I think people close to me have had to carry some of the consequences of me not being seen as dependable.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant periods in your life where these focus and impulsivity issues were not a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Not really — at least not in the way it’s been a problem recently. I mean, looking back, I can’t say I was ever naturally great at keeping myself on track, but the serious trouble part has really been since I was fired and lost the structure that used to hold things together for me.\n\nThat was about… a year and ten months ago now. Almost two years. Before that, there were systems around me — schedules, expectations, other people checking in, routines that kind of forced me to stay aligned. I don’t think I realized how much I depended on that until it was gone.\n\nSince then, it’s been much more obvious and much more damaging. Without those external supports, things started slipping in a way I couldn’t just explain away. So no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant period recently where it hasn’t been a problem. It’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months, ever since that firing and losing the systems that kept me on track.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties with focus and impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. My mother still brings it up, actually — kind of jokingly, but also in that way where you can tell it was a real thing at the time. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers before I was supposed to, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it.\n\nThat last part is the one that still makes me cringe a little, because it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done the work. Sometimes I had, but it would disappear somewhere between my desk, my backpack, home, and school. And I remember the adults around me acting like it was carelessness or laziness, like, “How can you do the assignment and then not turn it in?” Which is fair, I guess, but as a kid I didn’t really know how to explain it.\n\nSo yes, there were definitely comments from teachers, and my mother remembers them pretty clearly. At the time it just got framed as me being smart but not applying myself, or being disruptive, or needing to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently affect you throughout your childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. I mean, I can’t remember every year perfectly, obviously, but it wasn’t like it suddenly appeared as an adult. My mother still jokes about it because it showed up so much in grade school. She’ll say my report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the rest would be about me not staying in my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, or somehow losing homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that makes me feel like it was pretty consistent — it wasn’t just one bad teacher or one bad semester. It was kind of a theme. I was not a kid who just smoothly followed the classroom routine. Adults noticed it enough that it became part of how they described me. Even now, my mother can still repeat those comments back like they were a running joke, but honestly, it sounds like it was frustrating for everyone at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods in your childhood where you felt like you could focus and sit still without any issues?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really remember, no. I mean, I’m sure there were individual moments where I was absorbed in something or behaved fine for a while, but as a general thing in school, it doesn’t sound like I had long stretches where sitting still and focusing were just easy.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually — that my grade school report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it. So it wasn’t like there was this clear period where everyone thought, “Oh, he’s totally fine now.” It seems like it was a pretty steady theme.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months where you did not have any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in any way that feels like a clean break from it. Before that, I had systems around me that sort of compensated for it, even if I didn’t think of it that way at the time. Once those were gone, it became much harder to manage, and it started affecting me in a much bigger way. So I can’t point to a six-month period where I had no issues at all — just periods where the problems were more contained because I had enough external structure holding things together.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s definitely gotten more obvious as an adult, especially when I don’t have structure around me, but looking back, this has been there since I was a kid.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” — bright but doesn’t stay in his seat, bright but blurts out answers, bright but loses his homework. And the maddening part is, sometimes I had actually done the homework. I just wouldn’t turn it in, or I’d lose it somewhere between my desk, my backpack, and school. So it wasn’t like I suddenly became this way in midlife.\n\nAt the time, I think people just treated it like I was energetic or careless or not applying myself, because I could understand the material. But the focus and the impulse to speak before thinking — that was already there. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not just annoying or inconvenient — it’s been really distressing because it’s changed how people see me and what they’re willing to trust me with.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it feels like everything has gotten exposed. With freelance work, if I don’t follow through exactly when I should, or I let something slip, there isn’t really anyone else catching it. And I’ve lost clients because of that. People stop believing I’ll do what I say I’m going to do, even when I genuinely mean to.\n\nMy reputation has taken a real hit, and that bothers me a lot. I missed out on a senior service-manager role, too, and I keep thinking, “Would that have gone differently if people trusted me to stay on top of things?” That’s hard to sit with.\n\nAnd then at home, it’s not just my pride. My wife and I have had to worry about money because of it. That part really gets to me. It’s one thing for me to disappoint myself, but when it affects our stability and she has to live with the consequences too… yes, that causes me significant distress.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these issues when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I don’t remember ever being formally diagnosed with anything as a child, and I don’t think I received any specific treatment for it.\n\nBack then it was more handled as… I don’t know, teachers commenting on it, my parents being frustrated, people saying I needed to try harder or settle down. But I don’t recall anyone sitting down and saying, “This is a condition,” or sending me for therapy or medication or anything like that.\n\nIt was just sort of treated as part of my personality, I guess — like I was bright but careless, or restless, or not living up to my potential. So no, as far as I know, there wasn’t a diagnosis or treatment when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity affected your relationships with friends or family members in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, the biggest impact has been at home, with my wife, because the consequences don’t stay contained to me. Since I lost the structure of my old job and started doing more freelance work, people have stopped trusting me to follow through the way I say I will. And when that happens, it doesn’t just hurt my pride — it affects our money, our stability, and the way we talk to each other.\n\nI’ve lost freelance clients over this, and that was really hard. Not just financially, but because it feels like my reputation has taken a real hit. People start seeing me as unreliable, even when I’m not trying to be. I’ll genuinely mean to do something, and then if I don’t stay on top of it exactly right, the opportunity is gone or the person doesn’t come back.\n\nI also missed out on a senior service-manager role, and that one still bothers me. I keep thinking maybe if people had more confidence in my follow-through, it might have gone differently. And my wife has had to live with the fallout of that too — the stress, the uncertainty, worrying about money. So yes, it’s affected my family in a significant way. It’s embarrassing to say, but I think people close to me have had to carry some of the consequences of me not being seen as dependable.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant periods in your life where these focus and impulsivity issues were not a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Not really — at least not in the way it’s been a problem recently. I mean, looking back, I can’t say I was ever naturally great at keeping myself on track, but the serious trouble part has really been since I was fired and lost the structure that used to hold things together for me.\n\nThat was about… a year and ten months ago now. Almost two years. Before that, there were systems around me — schedules, expectations, other people checking in, routines that kind of forced me to stay aligned. I don’t think I realized how much I depended on that until it was gone.\n\nSince then, it’s been much more obvious and much more damaging. Without those external supports, things started slipping in a way I couldn’t just explain away. So no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant period recently where it hasn’t been a problem. It’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months, ever since that firing and losing the systems that kept me on track.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties with focus and impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. My mother still brings it up, actually — kind of jokingly, but also in that way where you can tell it was a real thing at the time. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers before I was supposed to, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it.\n\nThat last part is the one that still makes me cringe a little, because it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done the work. Sometimes I had, but it would disappear somewhere between my desk, my backpack, home, and school. And I remember the adults around me acting like it was carelessness or laziness, like, “How can you do the assignment and then not turn it in?” Which is fair, I guess, but as a kid I didn’t really know how to explain it.\n\nSo yes, there were definitely comments from teachers, and my mother remembers them pretty clearly. At the time it just got framed as me being smart but not applying myself, or being disruptive, or needing to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently affect you throughout your childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. I mean, I can’t remember every year perfectly, obviously, but it wasn’t like it suddenly appeared as an adult. My mother still jokes about it because it showed up so much in grade school. She’ll say my report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the rest would be about me not staying in my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, or somehow losing homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that makes me feel like it was pretty consistent — it wasn’t just one bad teacher or one bad semester. It was kind of a theme. I was not a kid who just smoothly followed the classroom routine. Adults noticed it enough that it became part of how they described me. Even now, my mother can still repeat those comments back like they were a running joke, but honestly, it sounds like it was frustrating for everyone at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods in your childhood where you felt like you could focus and sit still without any issues?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really remember, no. I mean, I’m sure there were individual moments where I was absorbed in something or behaved fine for a while, but as a general thing in school, it doesn’t sound like I had long stretches where sitting still and focusing were just easy.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually — that my grade school report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it. So it wasn’t like there was this clear period where everyone thought, “Oh, he’s totally fine now.” It seems like it was a pretty steady theme.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months where you did not have any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in any way that feels like a clean break from it. I think the more honest answer is that it’s always been there to some degree, but the part where it became seriously disruptive has been more recent.\n\nIt’s been causing me real trouble for about… a year and ten months now, I’d say. Before that, I had systems around me that sort of compensated for it, even if I didn’t think of it that way at the time. Once those were gone, it became much harder to manage, and it started affecting me in a much bigger way. So I can’t point to a six-month period where I had no issues at all — just periods where the problems were more contained because I had enough external structure holding things together.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in the way I wish it had, no. I think I’ve gotten better at hiding it at times, or building routines around it, but I wouldn’t say it significantly reduced with age.

If anything, the last almost two years have made it feel worse, or at least much harder to manage. It’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months now, basically since I was fired and lost the structure and systems that used to keep me on track. Before that, I could sort of lean on the job routine and expectations, even if it was still there underneath.

So I don’t think I “grew out of it.” I think I had supports that were doing more work than I realized, and when those were gone, it became very obvious.

It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive; I just feel kind of wound up if I’m sitting there with nothing moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. More than I’d like to admit, honestly. I catch myself cutting people off, especially when it’s anything technical. Someone will be describing a computer or network problem, and before they’ve even finished the second half of the sentence, my brain has already jumped to, “Oh, I know what that is,” and then I’m talking over them.\n\nIt’s not usually meant to be rude—I’m not trying to dominate the conversation—but the answer feels like it’s right there and I feel this pressure to say it before it disappears or before the conversation moves on. Sometimes I’ll even reach for the keyboard or mouse while they’re still explaining what happened, like I’m already trying to fix it before I’ve actually heard the full story.\n\nAnd then, of course, sometimes I’m wrong because I didn’t let them finish. That’s the part that makes me feel pretty bad. I know better. I work in support, so listening carefully is supposed to be part of the job. But in the moment I just jump in.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Honestly, that’s become one of the hardest parts of all of this, because it’s not just “I’m distracted at work” anymore. It’s affected how people see me.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I could kind of lean on the routines and systems around me. Since losing that structure, things have gotten a lot more obvious. I’ve been doing freelance work, and I’ve lost clients because I didn’t follow through the way I should have. Not because I didn’t want to, but because I’d miss something, or delay responding, or let a task sit too long, and then by the time I tried to recover it, the trust was already damaged.\n\nMy reputation has really taken a hit. People who used to think of me as reliable don’t really see me that way now, and that hurts. I also missed out on a senior service-manager role, and I can’t help feeling like this played a big part in it—people just don’t trust me to stay on top of things consistently.\n\nAnd at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because of it. That’s been… pretty upsetting. It’s one thing for me to feel frustrated with myself, but when it affects her too, and our stability, that makes me feel awful. So yes, outside of the actual job tasks, it’s caused real problems in daily life and in how people relate to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and impulsivity, or is this something that has developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not new. I mean, it’s definitely gotten more obvious as an adult, especially when I don’t have structure around me, but looking back, this has been there since I was a kid.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” — bright but doesn’t stay in his seat, bright but blurts out answers, bright but loses his homework. And the maddening part is, sometimes I had actually done the homework. I just wouldn’t turn it in, or I’d lose it somewhere between my desk, my backpack, and school. So it wasn’t like I suddenly became this way in midlife.\n\nAt the time, I think people just treated it like I was energetic or careless or not applying myself, because I could understand the material. But the focus and the impulse to speak before thinking — that was already there. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not just annoying or inconvenient — it’s been really distressing because it’s changed how people see me and what they’re willing to trust me with.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it feels like everything has gotten exposed. With freelance work, if I don’t follow through exactly when I should, or I let something slip, there isn’t really anyone else catching it. And I’ve lost clients because of that. People stop believing I’ll do what I say I’m going to do, even when I genuinely mean to.\n\nMy reputation has taken a real hit, and that bothers me a lot. I missed out on a senior service-manager role, too, and I keep thinking, “Would that have gone differently if people trusted me to stay on top of things?” That’s hard to sit with.\n\nAnd then at home, it’s not just my pride. My wife and I have had to worry about money because of it. That part really gets to me. It’s one thing for me to disappoint myself, but when it affects our stability and she has to live with the consequences too… yes, that causes me significant distress.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these issues when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I don’t remember ever being formally diagnosed with anything as a child, and I don’t think I received any specific treatment for it.\n\nBack then it was more handled as… I don’t know, teachers commenting on it, my parents being frustrated, people saying I needed to try harder or settle down. But I don’t recall anyone sitting down and saying, “This is a condition,” or sending me for therapy or medication or anything like that.\n\nIt was just sort of treated as part of my personality, I guess — like I was bright but careless, or restless, or not living up to my potential. So no, as far as I know, there wasn’t a diagnosis or treatment when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity affected your relationships with friends or family members in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, the biggest impact has been at home, with my wife, because the consequences don’t stay contained to me. Since I lost the structure of my old job and started doing more freelance work, people have stopped trusting me to follow through the way I say I will. And when that happens, it doesn’t just hurt my pride — it affects our money, our stability, and the way we talk to each other.\n\nI’ve lost freelance clients over this, and that was really hard. Not just financially, but because it feels like my reputation has taken a real hit. People start seeing me as unreliable, even when I’m not trying to be. I’ll genuinely mean to do something, and then if I don’t stay on top of it exactly right, the opportunity is gone or the person doesn’t come back.\n\nI also missed out on a senior service-manager role, and that one still bothers me. I keep thinking maybe if people had more confidence in my follow-through, it might have gone differently. And my wife has had to live with the fallout of that too — the stress, the uncertainty, worrying about money. So yes, it’s affected my family in a significant way. It’s embarrassing to say, but I think people close to me have had to carry some of the consequences of me not being seen as dependable.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant periods in your life where these focus and impulsivity issues were not a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Not really — at least not in the way it’s been a problem recently. I mean, looking back, I can’t say I was ever naturally great at keeping myself on track, but the serious trouble part has really been since I was fired and lost the structure that used to hold things together for me.\n\nThat was about… a year and ten months ago now. Almost two years. Before that, there were systems around me — schedules, expectations, other people checking in, routines that kind of forced me to stay aligned. I don’t think I realized how much I depended on that until it was gone.\n\nSince then, it’s been much more obvious and much more damaging. Without those external supports, things started slipping in a way I couldn’t just explain away. So no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant period recently where it hasn’t been a problem. It’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months, ever since that firing and losing the systems that kept me on track.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties with focus and impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. My mother still brings it up, actually — kind of jokingly, but also in that way where you can tell it was a real thing at the time. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers before I was supposed to, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it.\n\nThat last part is the one that still makes me cringe a little, because it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done the work. Sometimes I had, but it would disappear somewhere between my desk, my backpack, home, and school. And I remember the adults around me acting like it was carelessness or laziness, like, “How can you do the assignment and then not turn it in?” Which is fair, I guess, but as a kid I didn’t really know how to explain it.\n\nSo yes, there were definitely comments from teachers, and my mother remembers them pretty clearly. At the time it just got framed as me being smart but not applying myself, or being disruptive, or needing to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently affect you throughout your childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. I mean, I can’t remember every year perfectly, obviously, but it wasn’t like it suddenly appeared as an adult. My mother still jokes about it because it showed up so much in grade school. She’ll say my report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the rest would be about me not staying in my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, or somehow losing homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that makes me feel like it was pretty consistent — it wasn’t just one bad teacher or one bad semester. It was kind of a theme. I was not a kid who just smoothly followed the classroom routine. Adults noticed it enough that it became part of how they described me. Even now, my mother can still repeat those comments back like they were a running joke, but honestly, it sounds like it was frustrating for everyone at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods in your childhood where you felt like you could focus and sit still without any issues?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really remember, no. I mean, I’m sure there were individual moments where I was absorbed in something or behaved fine for a while, but as a general thing in school, it doesn’t sound like I had long stretches where sitting still and focusing were just easy.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually — that my grade school report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it. So it wasn’t like there was this clear period where everyone thought, “Oh, he’s totally fine now.” It seems like it was a pretty steady theme.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months where you did not have any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in any way that feels like a clean break from it. I think the more honest answer is that it’s always been there to some degree, but the part where it became seriously disruptive has been more recent.\n\nIt’s been causing me real trouble for about… a year and ten months now, I’d say. That lines up with when I was fired and lost the structure that had been keeping me on track. Before that, I had systems around me that sort of compensated for it, even if I didn’t think of it that way at the time. Once those were gone, it became much harder to manage, and it started affecting me in a much bigger way. So I can’t point to a six-month period where I had no issues at all — just periods where the problems were more contained because I had enough external structure holding things together.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely not just one place. It’s probably most obvious with work, because that’s where the consequences have been the clearest, but it spills over at home too.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, things have kind of unraveled in a way that’s embarrassing to talk about. My reputation has taken a real hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because I didn’t follow through reliably enough, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I really thought I should have been in the running for. And at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because people just don’t trust me the way they used to to get things done.\n\nSo, yes — work, home, and honestly the social/professional side too, because once people start seeing you as unreliable, it follows you around.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes, from what I understand, it was there pretty early — definitely in grade school. I don’t remember every detail myself, obviously, but my mother still brings it up and kind of jokes about it, like it was this recurring theme on my report cards.\n\nThey’d say I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers instead of waiting my turn. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which is still frustrating to hear because it sounds so familiar. So yes, I think before 12, there were already pretty clear signs.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods as an adult where your symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity seemed to disappear or significantly diminish for a while?\n\nPatient: Not disappear, no. I mean… there were definitely times as an adult when it was more manageable, but looking back, I think that had more to do with having systems around me than the symptoms actually going away.\n\nThe big change was after I was fired. That was about a year and ten months ago, and since then it’s been causing serious trouble. Before that, the structure of work, routines, other people expecting things from me in a predictable way — all of that kind of held me in place. Once that was gone, it became a lot more obvious how much I was depending on those supports.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where it really disappeared. It was more like it was contained for a while, and then when those systems were gone, it got much harder to keep things together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant reduction in your symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity as you've grown older?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way I wish it had, no. I think I’ve gotten better at hiding it at times, or building routines around it, but I wouldn’t say it significantly reduced with age.\n\nIf anything, the last almost two years have made it feel worse, or at least much harder to manage. It’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months now, basically since I was fired and lost the structure and systems that used to keep me on track. Before that, I could sort of lean on the job routine and expectations, even if it was still there underneath.\n\nSo I don’t think I “grew out of it.” I think I had supports that were doing more work than I realized, and when those were gone, it became very obvious.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t remember ever being formally diagnosed with anything as a child, and I don’t think I received any specific treatment for it.\n\nBack then it was more handled as… I don’t know, teachers commenting on it, my parents being frustrated, people saying I needed to try harder or settle down. But I don’t recall anyone sitting down and saying, “This is a condition,” or sending me for therapy or medication or anything like that.\n\nIt was just sort of treated as part of my personality, I guess — like I was bright but careless, or restless, or not living up to my potential. It’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months, ever since that firing and losing the systems that kept me on track.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties with focus and impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. Even now, my mother can still repeat those comments back like they were a running joke, but honestly, it sounds like it was frustrating for everyone at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods in your childhood where you felt like you could focus and sit still without any issues?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really remember, no. I mean, I’m sure there were individual moments where I was absorbed in something or behaved fine for a while, but as a general thing in school, it doesn’t sound like I had long stretches where sitting still and focusing were just easy.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually — that my grade school report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it. So it wasn’t like there was this clear period where everyone thought, “Oh, he’s totally fine now.” It seems like it was a pretty steady theme.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months where you did not have any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in any way that feels like a clean break from it. I think the more honest answer is that it’s always been there to some degree, but the part where it became seriously disruptive has been more recent.\n\nIt’s been causing me real trouble for about… a year and ten months now, I’d say. That lines up with when I was fired and lost the structure that had been keeping me on track. Before that, I had systems around me that sort of compensated for it, even if I didn’t think of it that way at the time. Once those were gone, it became much harder to manage, and it started affecting me in a much bigger way. So I can’t point to a six-month period where I had no issues at all — just periods where the problems were more contained because I had enough external structure holding things together.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely not just one place. It’s probably most obvious with work, because that’s where the consequences have been the clearest, but it spills over at home too.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, things have kind of unraveled in a way that’s embarrassing to talk about. My reputation has taken a real hit. I’ve lost freelance clients because I didn’t follow through reliably enough, and I missed out on a senior service-manager role that I really thought I should have been in the running for. And at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because people just don’t trust me the way they used to to get things done.\n\nSo, yes — work, home, and honestly the social/professional side too, because once people start seeing you as unreliable, it follows you around.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Yes, from what I understand, it was there pretty early — definitely in grade school. I don’t remember every detail myself, obviously, but my mother still brings it up and kind of jokes about it, like it was this recurring theme on my report cards.\n\nThey’d say I was bright, but I wouldn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers instead of waiting my turn. And apparently I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it, which is still frustrating to hear because it sounds so familiar. So yes, I think before 12, there were already pretty clear signs.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any periods as an adult where your symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity seemed to disappear or significantly diminish for a while?\n\nPatient: Not disappear, no. I mean… there were definitely times as an adult when it was more manageable, but looking back, I think that had more to do with having systems around me than the symptoms actually going away.\n\nThe big change was after I was fired. That was about a year and ten months ago, and since then it’s been causing serious trouble. Before that, the structure of work, routines, other people expecting things from me in a predictable way — all of that kind of held me in place. Once that was gone, it became a lot more obvious how much I was depending on those supports.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where it really disappeared. It was more like it was contained for a while, and then when those systems were gone, it got much harder to keep things together.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant reduction in your symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity as you've grown older?\n\nPatient: Not really in the way I wish it had, no. I think I’ve gotten better at hiding it at times, or building routines around it, but I wouldn’t say it significantly reduced with age.\n\nIf anything, the last almost two years have made it feel worse, or at least much harder to manage. It’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months now, basically since I was fired and lost the structure and systems that used to keep me on track. Before that, I could sort of lean on the job routine and expectations, even if it was still there underneath.\n\nSo I don’t think I “grew out of it.” I think I had supports that were doing more work than I realized, and when those were gone, it became very obvious.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity consistently over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very consistently, unfortunately. It’s been… not just the past year, but about a year and ten months now where it’s been causing serious trouble.\n\nThat really lines up with when I was fired and lost the structure I had around me. Before that, I had systems — work expectations, routines, other people kind of anchoring the day — and once those were gone, I realized how much I’d been leaning on them. Since then, it hasn’t felt like something that comes and goes in any meaningful way. It’s been pretty constant, and it’s affected me a lot.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Once that was gone, it became a lot more obvious how much I was depending on those supports.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there was a period where it really disappeared. Before that, I could sort of lean on the job routine and expectations, even if it was still there underneath.\n\nSo I don’t think I “grew out of it.” I think I had supports that were doing more work than I realized, and when those were gone, it became very obvious.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity consistently over the past year?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s gotten to where even important tasks at work are slipping away from me while I’m doing them.\n\nI do computer support, so a lot of my job is tracing problems carefully—looking through logs, following a network issue step by step, keeping track of what I’ve ruled out. And I’ll sit down to work on a client’s network problem, fully intending to focus, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve been staring at the same log entries over and over. Like, I’ve reread the same lines three times, but I still can’t tell you what they actually say or what the next step is supposed to be.\n\nIt’s not that I don’t care. That’s the frustrating part. These are things that matter, and I know they matter while I’m doing them, but my attention just won’t stay put long enough for me to get through the task.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless, like you can't sit still?\n\nPatient: Yes, very often. It’s almost embarrassing how automatic it is. If I’m sitting with a client and I’m supposed to be listening or explaining something, I’ll suddenly realize my leg has been bouncing the whole time under the desk. Or I’m clicking a pen without even noticing I picked it up.\n\nAnd it happens in meetings too, like at chess club meetings—I’ll be trying to sit still and pay attention, but my hands need something to do. I’ll end up turning a little cable tie or some random bit of plastic in my fingers over and over until someone gives me that look, like, “Can you stop?” Then I’ll realize I’ve been doing it nonstop. It’s not just annoying or inconvenient — it’s been really distressing because it’s changed how people see me and what they’re willing to trust me with.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it feels like everything has gotten exposed. With freelance work, if I don’t follow through exactly when I should, or I let something slip, there isn’t really anyone else catching it. And I’ve lost clients because of that. People stop believing I’ll do what I say I’m going to do, even when I genuinely mean to.\n\nMy reputation has taken a real hit, and that bothers me a lot. I don’t remember ever being formally diagnosed with anything as a child, and I don’t think I received any specific treatment for it.\n\nBack then it was more handled as… I don’t know, teachers commenting on it, my parents being frustrated, people saying I needed to try harder or settle down. But I don’t recall anyone sitting down and saying, “This is a condition,” or sending me for therapy or medication or anything like that.\n\nIt was just sort of treated as part of my personality, I guess — like I was bright but careless, or restless, or not living up to my potential. It’s not like I’m trying to be disruptive; I just feel kind of wound up if I’m sitting there with nothing moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often interrupt others when they're speaking?\n\nPatient: Yes. Someone will be describing a computer or network problem, and before they’ve even finished the second half of the sentence, my brain has already jumped to, “Oh, I know what that is,” and then I’m talking over them.\n\nIt’s not usually meant to be rude—I’m not trying to dominate the conversation—but the answer feels like it’s right there and I feel this pressure to say it before it disappears or before the conversation moves on. Sometimes I’ll even reach for the keyboard or mouse while they’re still explaining what happened, like I’m already trying to fix it before I’ve actually heard the full story.\n\nAnd then, of course, sometimes I’m wrong because I didn’t let them finish. That’s the part that makes me feel pretty bad. I know better. I work in support, so listening carefully is supposed to be part of the job. But in the moment I just jump in.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity caused problems in your personal relationships or daily life outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Honestly, that’s become one of the hardest parts of all of this, because it’s not just “I’m distracted at work” anymore. It’s affected how people see me.\n\nWhen I had the structure of my old job, I could kind of lean on the routines and systems around me. Since losing that structure, things have gotten a lot more obvious. I’ve been doing freelance work, and I’ve lost clients because I didn’t follow through the way I should have. Not because I didn’t want to, but because I’d miss something, or delay responding, or let a task sit too long, and then by the time I tried to recover it, the trust was already damaged.\n\nMy reputation has really taken a hit. People who used to think of me as reliable don’t really see me that way now, and that hurts. I also missed out on a senior service-manager role, and I can’t help feeling like this played a big part in it—people just don’t trust me to stay on top of things consistently.\n\nAnd at home, my wife and I have had to worry about money because of it. That’s been… pretty upsetting. It’s one thing for me to feel frustrated with myself, but when it affects her too, and our stability, that makes me feel awful. So yes, outside of the actual job tasks, it’s caused real problems in daily life and in how people relate to me.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had difficulty with focus and impulsivity, or is this something that has developed more recently in your life?\n\nPatient: No, it’s not new. I mean, it’s definitely gotten more obvious as an adult, especially when I don’t have structure around me, but looking back, this has been there since I was a kid.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” — bright but doesn’t stay in his seat, bright but blurts out answers, bright but loses his homework. And the maddening part is, sometimes I had actually done the homework. I just wouldn’t turn it in, or I’d lose it somewhere between my desk, my backpack, and school. So it wasn’t like I suddenly became this way in midlife.\n\nAt the time, I think people just treated it like I was energetic or careless or not applying myself, because I could understand the material. But the focus and the impulse to speak before thinking — that was already there. It’s just that now the consequences are bigger.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your difficulties with focus and impulsivity are causing you significant distress?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s not just annoying or inconvenient — it’s been really distressing because it’s changed how people see me and what they’re willing to trust me with.\n\nSince I lost the structure of my old job, it feels like everything has gotten exposed. With freelance work, if I don’t follow through exactly when I should, or I let something slip, there isn’t really anyone else catching it. And I’ve lost clients because of that. People stop believing I’ll do what I say I’m going to do, even when I genuinely mean to.\n\nMy reputation has taken a real hit, and that bothers me a lot. I missed out on a senior service-manager role, too, and I keep thinking, “Would that have gone differently if people trusted me to stay on top of things?” That’s hard to sit with.\n\nAnd then at home, it’s not just my pride. My wife and I have had to worry about money because of it. That part really gets to me. It’s one thing for me to disappoint myself, but when it affects our stability and she has to live with the consequences too… yes, that causes me significant distress.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any diagnoses or receive any treatment for these issues when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not that I know of, no. I don’t remember ever being formally diagnosed with anything as a child, and I don’t think I received any specific treatment for it.\n\nBack then it was more handled as… I don’t know, teachers commenting on it, my parents being frustrated, people saying I needed to try harder or settle down. But I don’t recall anyone sitting down and saying, “This is a condition,” or sending me for therapy or medication or anything like that.\n\nIt was just sort of treated as part of my personality, I guess — like I was bright but careless, or restless, or not living up to my potential. So no, as far as I know, there wasn’t a diagnosis or treatment when I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with focus and impulsivity affected your relationships with friends or family members in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. I mean, the biggest impact has been at home, with my wife, because the consequences don’t stay contained to me. Since I lost the structure of my old job and started doing more freelance work, people have stopped trusting me to follow through the way I say I will. And when that happens, it doesn’t just hurt my pride — it affects our money, our stability, and the way we talk to each other.\n\nI’ve lost freelance clients over this, and that was really hard. Not just financially, but because it feels like my reputation has taken a real hit. People start seeing me as unreliable, even when I’m not trying to be. I’ll genuinely mean to do something, and then if I don’t stay on top of it exactly right, the opportunity is gone or the person doesn’t come back.\n\nI also missed out on a senior service-manager role, and that one still bothers me. I keep thinking maybe if people had more confidence in my follow-through, it might have gone differently. And my wife has had to live with the fallout of that too — the stress, the uncertainty, worrying about money. So yes, it’s affected my family in a significant way. It’s embarrassing to say, but I think people close to me have had to carry some of the consequences of me not being seen as dependable.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant periods in your life where these focus and impulsivity issues were not a problem for you?\n\nPatient: Not really — at least not in the way it’s been a problem recently. I mean, looking back, I can’t say I was ever naturally great at keeping myself on track, but the serious trouble part has really been since I was fired and lost the structure that used to hold things together for me.\n\nThat was about… a year and ten months ago now. Almost two years. Before that, there were systems around me — schedules, expectations, other people checking in, routines that kind of forced me to stay aligned. I don’t think I realized how much I depended on that until it was gone.\n\nSince then, it’s been much more obvious and much more damaging. Without those external supports, things started slipping in a way I couldn’t just explain away. So no, I wouldn’t say there’s been a significant period recently where it hasn’t been a problem. It’s been causing serious trouble for about a year and ten months, ever since that firing and losing the systems that kept me on track.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever comment on your behavior or difficulties with focus and impulsivity when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, they did. My mother still brings it up, actually — kind of jokingly, but also in that way where you can tell it was a real thing at the time. She’ll say my grade school report cards were always some version of, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers before I was supposed to, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it.\n\nThat last part is the one that still makes me cringe a little, because it wasn’t always that I hadn’t done the work. Sometimes I had, but it would disappear somewhere between my desk, my backpack, home, and school. And I remember the adults around me acting like it was carelessness or laziness, like, “How can you do the assignment and then not turn it in?” Which is fair, I guess, but as a kid I didn’t really know how to explain it.\n\nSo yes, there were definitely comments from teachers, and my mother remembers them pretty clearly. At the time it just got framed as me being smart but not applying myself, or being disruptive, or needing to settle down.\n\nDoctor: Did your difficulties with focus and impulsivity consistently affect you throughout your childhood?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it did. I mean, I can’t remember every year perfectly, obviously, but it wasn’t like it suddenly appeared as an adult. My mother still jokes about it because it showed up so much in grade school. She’ll say my report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the rest would be about me not staying in my seat, calling out answers before I was supposed to, or somehow losing homework even when I’d actually done it.\n\nAnd that’s the part that makes me feel like it was pretty consistent — it wasn’t just one bad teacher or one bad semester. It was kind of a theme. I was not a kid who just smoothly followed the classroom routine. Adults noticed it enough that it became part of how they described me. Even now, my mother can still repeat those comments back like they were a running joke, but honestly, it sounds like it was frustrating for everyone at the time.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any periods in your childhood where you felt like you could focus and sit still without any issues?\n\nPatient: Not that I can really remember, no. I mean, I’m sure there were individual moments where I was absorbed in something or behaved fine for a while, but as a general thing in school, it doesn’t sound like I had long stretches where sitting still and focusing were just easy.\n\nMy mother still jokes about it, actually — that my grade school report cards were always basically, “He’s bright, but…” and then the “but” was that I didn’t stay in my seat, or I’d blurt out answers, or I’d lose homework even when I had actually done it. So it wasn’t like there was this clear period where everyone thought, “Oh, he’s totally fine now.” It seems like it was a pretty steady theme.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever experienced a period of at least six months where you did not have any symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity?\n\nPatient: Not really, no — at least not in any way that feels like a clean break from it. I think the more honest answer is that it’s always been there to some degree, but the part where it became seriously disruptive has been more recent.\n\nIt’s been causing me real trouble for about… a year and ten months now, I’d say. That lines up with when I was fired and lost the structure that had been keeping me on track. Before that, I had systems around me that sort of compensated for it, even if I didn’t think of it that way at the time. Once those were gone, it became much harder to manage, and it started affecting me in a much bigger way. So I can’t point to a six-month period where I had no issues at all — just periods where the problems were more contained because I had enough external structure holding things together.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant symptoms of inattention or hyperactivity in two or more settings, such as at home, in social situations, and at work?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s definitely not just one place.
